          <h2>
            <span class="mw-headline" id="Development_of_the_outbreak"
              >Development of the outbreak</span
            >
          </h2>
          <h3>
            <span class="mw-headline" id="Initial_outbreak_in_Guinea"
              >Initial outbreak in Guinea</span
            >
          </h3>
          <p>
            Researchers believe that the first human case of the
            <a href="/wiki/Ebola_virus_disease" title="Ebola virus disease"
              >Ebola virus disease</a
            >
            leading to the 2014 outbreak was a 2-year-old boy who died 6
            December 2013 in the village of
            <a href="/wiki/Meliandou" title="Meliandou">Meliandou</a>,
            <a
              href="/wiki/Gu%C3%A9ck%C3%A9dou_Prefecture"
              title="Guéckédou Prefecture"
              >Guéckédou Prefecture</a
            >, <a href="/wiki/Guinea" title="Guinea">Guinea</a>. His mother,
            3-year-old sister and grandmother then became ill with symptoms
            consistent with Ebola infection and died. People infected by those
            victims spread the disease to other villages.<sup
              id="cite_ref-Tracing_Ebola.E2.80.99s_Breakout_to_an_African_2-Year-Old_2-1"
              class="reference"
              ><a
                href="#cite_note-Tracing_Ebola.E2.80.99s_Breakout_to_an_African_2-Year-Old-2"
                ><span>[</span>2<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-Baize-2014_14-0" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-Baize-2014-14"
                ><span>[</span>14<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On 19 March, the Ministry of Health acknowledged a local outbreak of
            an undetermined
            <a
              href="/wiki/Viral_hemorrhagic_fever"
              title="Viral hemorrhagic fever"
              >viral hemorrhagic fever</a
            >; the outbreak, ongoing since February, had sickened at least 35
            people and killed 23. Ebola was suspected, and samples had been sent
            to <a href="/wiki/Senegal" title="Senegal">Senegal</a> and
            <a href="/wiki/France" title="France">France</a> for disease
            identification.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-15"><span>[</span>15<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            On 25 March, the
            <a
              href="/wiki/World_Health_Organization"
              title="World Health Organization"
              >World Health Organization</a
            >
            (WHO) reported that the Ministry of Health of Guinea had reported an
            outbreak of Ebola virus disease in four southeastern districts:
            Guekedou, Macenta, Nzerekore and Kissidougou with suspected cases in
            the neighbouring countries of
            <a href="/wiki/Liberia" title="Liberia">Liberia</a> and
            <a href="/wiki/Sierra_Leone" title="Sierra Leone">Sierra Leone</a>
            being investigated. In Guinea, a total of 86 suspected cases,
            including 59 deaths (case fatality ratio: 68.5%), had been reported
            as of 24 March.<sup
              id="cite_ref-Previous_Updates_2014_West_Africa_Outbreak_16-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a
                href="#cite_note-Previous_Updates_2014_West_Africa_Outbreak-16"
                ><span>[</span>16<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On 31 March, the US
            <a
              href="/wiki/Center_for_Disease_Control_and_Prevention"
              title="Center for Disease Control and Prevention"
              class="mw-redirect"
              >Center for Disease Control and Prevention</a
            >
            (CDC) sent a five-person team to assist Guinea Ministry of Health
            and World Health Organization to lead an international response to
            the Ebola outbreak. On that date the WHO reported 112 suspected and
            confirmed cases including 70 deaths. Two cases were reported from
            Liberia of people who had recently travelled to Guinea, and
            suspected cases in Liberia and Sierra Leone were being
            investigated.<sup
              id="cite_ref-Previous_Updates_2014_West_Africa_Outbreak_16-1"
              class="reference"
              ><a
                href="#cite_note-Previous_Updates_2014_West_Africa_Outbreak-16"
                ><span>[</span>16<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            On 30 April, Guinea's Ministry of Health reported 221 suspected and
            confirmed cases including 146 deaths. The cases included 25 health
            care workers with 16 deaths. By late May, the outbreak had spread to
            <a href="/wiki/Conakry" title="Conakry">Conakry</a>, Guinea's
            capital, a city of about two million inhabitants.<sup
              id="cite_ref-Previous_Updates_2014_West_Africa_Outbreak_16-2"
              class="reference"
              ><a
                href="#cite_note-Previous_Updates_2014_West_Africa_Outbreak-16"
                ><span>[</span>16<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            On 28 May, the total cases reported had reached 281 with 186
            deaths.<sup
              id="cite_ref-Previous_Updates_2014_West_Africa_Outbreak_16-3"
              class="reference"
              ><a
                href="#cite_note-Previous_Updates_2014_West_Africa_Outbreak-16"
                ><span>[</span>16<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <h3>
            <span class="mw-headline" id="Subsequent_spread"
              >Subsequent spread</span
            >
          </h3>
          <p>
            In <a href="/wiki/Liberia" title="Liberia">Liberia</a>, the disease
            was reported in
            <a href="/wiki/Lofa_County" title="Lofa County">Lofa</a> and
            <a href="/wiki/Nimba_County" title="Nimba County">Nimba</a> counties
            in late March,<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-17"><span>[</span>17<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            and by mid-April, the Ministry of Health and Social Welfare had
            recorded possible cases in
            <a href="/wiki/Margibi_County" title="Margibi County">Margibi</a>
            and
            <a href="/wiki/Montserrado_County" title="Montserrado County"
              >Montserrado</a
            >
            counties.<sup id="cite_ref-mohsw_18-0" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-mohsw-18"
                ><span>[</span>18<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            In mid-June the first cases in Liberia's capital
            <a href="/wiki/Monrovia" title="Monrovia">Monrovia</a> were
            reported.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-19"><span>[</span>19<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            <a href="/wiki/Sierra_Leone" title="Sierra Leone">Sierra Leone</a>,
            <a href="/wiki/Mali" title="Mali">Mali</a>, and
            <a href="/wiki/Ghana" title="Ghana">Ghana</a> identified suspected
            cases of the disease by mid-April, but all
            <a
              href="/wiki/Clinical_samples"
              title="Clinical samples"
              class="mw-redirect"
              >clinical samples</a
            >
            of suspected cases at the time tested negative for the Ebola
            virus.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-20"><span>[</span>20<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            In Guinea, from 23 to 27 May, three previously affected districts
            (Guéckédou, <a href="/wiki/Macenta" title="Macenta">Macenta</a> and
            Conakry), and four new districts (Boffa, Télimélé, Boke and
            <a href="/wiki/Dubr%C3%A9ka" title="Dubréka">Dubréka</a>) reported
            cases.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-21"><span>[</span>21<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            The outbreak progressed rapidly in Sierra Leone. The first cases
            were reported on 25 May in the
            <a href="/wiki/Kailahun_District" title="Kailahun District"
              >Kailahun District</a
            >, near the border with Guéckédou in Guinea.<sup
              id="cite_ref-22"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-22"><span>[</span>22<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            By 20 June, there were 158 suspected cases, mainly in Kailahun and
            the adjacent district of
            <a href="/wiki/Kenema_District" title="Kenema District">Kenema</a>,
            but also in the
            <a href="/wiki/Kambia_District" title="Kambia District">Kambia</a>,
            <a href="/wiki/Port_Loko_District" title="Port Loko District"
              >Port Loko</a
            >
            and
            <a
              href="/wiki/Western_Area_Rural_District"
              title="Western Area Rural District"
              >Western</a
            >
            districts in the north west of the country.<sup
              id="cite_ref-23"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-23"><span>[</span>23<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            By 17 July, the total number of suspected cases in the country stood
            at 442, and had overtaken those in Guinea and Liberia.<sup
              id="cite_ref-WHO_18_July_2014_24-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-WHO_18_July_2014-24"
                ><span>[</span>24<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            By 20 July, cases of the disease had additionally been reported in
            the
            <a href="/wiki/Bo_District" title="Bo District">Bo District</a>;<sup
              id="cite_ref-25"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-25"><span>[</span>25<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            the first case in
            <a href="/wiki/Freetown" title="Freetown">Freetown</a>, Sierra
            Leone's capital, was reported in late July.<sup
              id="cite_ref-BBC_Freetown_26-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-BBC_Freetown-26"
                ><span>[</span>26<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-27"><span>[</span>27<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            Médecins Sans Frontières described the situation as being "totally
            out of control" in late June. Urging the world to offer aid to the
            affected regions, the Director-General said, "Countries affected to
            date simply do not have the capacity to manage an outbreak of this
            size and complexity on their own. I urge the international community
            to provide this support on the most urgent basis possible."<sup
              id="cite_ref-Control_28-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-Control-28"
                ><span>[</span>28<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            The first case in
            <a href="/wiki/Nigeria" title="Nigeria">Nigeria</a> was reported by
            the WHO on 25 July:<sup
              id="cite_ref-WHO_25_July_2014_29-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-WHO_25_July_2014-29"
                ><span>[</span>29<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            Patrick Sawyer, a Liberian
            <a
              href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Finance"
              title="Ministry of Finance"
              class="mw-redirect"
              >Ministry of Finance</a
            >
            official, flew from Liberia to Nigeria after exposure to the virus,
            and died at <a href="/wiki/Lagos" title="Lagos">Lagos</a> soon after
            arrival.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-30"><span>[</span>30<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            In response, the hospital where he was being treated was shut down
            and quarantined, and the health officials who were treating him were
            isolated in an attempt to stop the spread of the virus.<sup
              id="cite_ref-31"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-31"><span>[</span>31<span>]</span></a></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-BBC_32-0" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-BBC-32"
                ><span>[</span>32<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-33"><span>[</span>33<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            However, a doctor and nurse who treated Sawyer both died from
            Ebola.<sup id="cite_ref-reuters1_34-0" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-reuters1-34"
                ><span>[</span>34<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-theguardian1_35-0" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-theguardian1-35"
                ><span>[</span>35<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <div class="thumb tright">
            <div class="thumbinner" style="width: 222px">
              <a
                href="/wiki/File:2014_West_Africa_Ebola_virus_outbreak_situation_map.jpg"
                class="image"
                ><img
                  alt=""
                  src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/2014_West_Africa_Ebola_virus_outbreak_situation_map.jpg/220px-2014_West_Africa_Ebola_virus_outbreak_situation_map.jpg"
                  width="220"
                  height="259"
                  class="thumbimage"
                  srcset="
                    //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/2014_West_Africa_Ebola_virus_outbreak_situation_map.jpg/330px-2014_West_Africa_Ebola_virus_outbreak_situation_map.jpg 1.5x,
                    //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/2014_West_Africa_Ebola_virus_outbreak_situation_map.jpg/440px-2014_West_Africa_Ebola_virus_outbreak_situation_map.jpg 2x
                  "
                  data-file-width="1200"
                  data-file-height="1414"
              /></a>
              <div class="thumbcaption">
                <div class="magnify">
                  <a
                    href="/wiki/File:2014_West_Africa_Ebola_virus_outbreak_situation_map.jpg"
                    class="internal"
                    title="Enlarge"
                  ></a>
                </div>
                Situation on Aug 14, 2014.<sup
                  id="cite_ref-36"
                  class="reference"
                  ><a href="#cite_note-36"
                    ><span>[</span>36<span>]</span></a
                  ></sup
                >
              </div>
            </div>
          </div>
          <p>
            In August, a
            <a href="/wiki/Cordon_sanitaire" title="Cordon sanitaire"
              >cordon sanitaire</a
            >, a disease fighting practice that forcibly isolates affected
            regions, was established in the triangular area where Guinea,
            Liberia, and Sierra Leone are separated only by porous borders and
            where 70 percent of the known cases had been found.<sup
              id="cite_ref-cordon_8-1"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-cordon-8"
                ><span>[</span>8<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            Disease reports accelerated in August with 40% of the total cases
            reported in a period of only three weeks. The WHO stated that the
            acceleration could see the number of cases reported exceed
            20,000.<sup id="cite_ref-EbolaAcceleration_37-0" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-EbolaAcceleration-37"
                ><span>[</span>37<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On 29 August, the
            <a href="/wiki/Senegalese" title="Senegalese" class="mw-redirect"
              >Senegalese</a
            >
            Health minister, Awa Marie Coll Seck, announced the first case of
            Ebola in the country. The case has been confirmed in Senegal.<sup
              id="cite_ref-2014_Ebola_Senegal_38-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-2014_Ebola_Senegal-38"
                ><span>[</span>38<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-2014_Ebola_Senegal_1_39-0" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-2014_Ebola_Senegal_1-39"
                ><span>[</span>39<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <h2>
            <span class="mw-headline" id="Complications_in_containment_efforts"
              >Complications in containment efforts</span
            >
          </h2>
          <p>
            Difficulties faced in attempting to contain the outbreak include the
            outbreak's multiple locations across country borders,<sup
              id="cite_ref-Control_28-1"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-Control-28"
                ><span>[</span>28<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            inadequate equipment provided for medical personnel,<sup
              id="cite_ref-40"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-40"><span>[</span>40<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            and lack of soap and water for hand-washing and disinfection.<sup
              id="cite_ref-41"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-41"><span>[</span>41<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            Containment efforts are further hindered because there is reluctance
            among country people to recognize the danger of infection related to
            person-to-person spread of disease, such as burial practices which
            include washing of the body of one that has died.<sup
              id="cite_ref-BBC_Freetown_26-1"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-BBC_Freetown-26"
                ><span>[</span>26<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-42"><span>[</span>42<span>]</span></a></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-43"><span>[</span>43<span>]</span></a></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-44"><span>[</span>44<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            See also
            <a
              href="/wiki/Ebola_virus_disease#Infection_control_and_containment"
              title="Ebola virus disease"
              >Control and Containment</a
            >.
          </p>
          <h3>
            <span class="mw-headline" id="Extreme_poverty"
              >Extreme poverty</span
            >
          </h3>
          <p>
            A condition of dire poverty exists in many of the areas that have
            experienced a high incidence of infections. According to the
            director of
            <a href="/wiki/NGO" title="NGO" class="mw-redirect">NGO</a>
            <a
              href="/wiki/Plan_(aid_organisation)"
              title="Plan (aid organisation)"
              >Plan International</a
            >
            in Guinea, "The poor
            <a
              href="/wiki/Living_conditions"
              title="Living conditions"
              class="mw-redirect"
              >living conditions</a
            >
            and lack of
            <a href="/wiki/Water_supply" title="Water supply">water</a> and
            <a href="/wiki/Sanitation" title="Sanitation">sanitation</a> in most
            districts of Conakry pose a serious risk that the epidemic escalates
            into a crisis. People do not think to
            <a href="/wiki/Hand_washing" title="Hand washing"
              >wash their hands</a
            >
            when they do not have enough
            <a href="/wiki/Drinking_water" title="Drinking water"
              >water to drink</a
            >."<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-45"><span>[</span>45<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
          </p>
          <h3>
            <span class="mw-headline" id="Rumours_and_denial"
              >Rumours and denial</span
            >
          </h3>
          <p>
            Denial in some affected countries has often made containment efforts
            difficult.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-46"><span>[</span>46<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            Language barriers and the appearance of medical teams in protective
            suits has sometimes exaggerated fears of the virus.<sup
              id="cite_ref-47"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-47"><span>[</span>47<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            There are reports that some people believe that the disease is
            caused by
            <a href="/wiki/Witchcraft" title="Witchcraft">sorcery</a> and that
            doctors are killing patients.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-48"><span>[</span>48<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            In late July, the former Liberian health minister, Peter Coleman,
            stated that "people don't seem to believe anything the government
            now says."<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_28_July_2014_49-0" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-BBC_28_July_2014-49"
                ><span>[</span>49<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            Acting on a rumor that the virus was invented to conceal
            "cannibalistic rituals", demonstrations were staged outside of the
            main hospital treating Ebola patients in
            <a href="/wiki/Kenema" title="Kenema">Kenema</a>, Sierra Leone. The
            demonstrations were broken up by the police and resulted in the need
            to use armed guards at the hospital.<sup
              id="cite_ref-50"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-50"><span>[</span>50<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            In Liberia, a mob attacked an Ebola isolation centre stealing
            equipment and "freeing" patients while shouting "There's no
            Ebola".<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-51"><span>[</span>51<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            Red Cross staff was forced to suspend operations in southeast Guinea
            after they were threatened by a group of men armed with knives.<sup
              id="cite_ref-52"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-52"><span>[</span>52<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
          </p>
          <h3>
            <span class="mw-headline" id="Healthcare_workers"
              >Healthcare workers</span
            >
          </h3>
          <p>
            Healthcare providers caring for Ebola patients and family and
            friends in close contact with Ebola patients are at the highest risk
            of getting sick because they may come in direct contact with the
            blood or body fluids of sick patients. In some places affected by
            the current outbreak, care may be provided in clinics with limited
            resources (for example, no running water, no climate control, no
            floors, inadequate medical supplies), and workers could be in those
            areas for several hours with a number of Ebola infected
            patients.<sup id="cite_ref-CDC_53-0" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-CDC-53"
                ><span>[</span>53<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            As of 11 August, healthcare workers have represented nearly 10
            percent of the cases and fatalities, significantly impairing the
            ability to respond to the outbreak in a country which already faces
            a severe shortage of doctors.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-54"><span>[</span>54<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            On 26 August, the WHO reported that more than 240 health care
            workers have developed Ebola in Guinea, Liberia, Nigeria, and Sierra
            Leone, and more than 120 have died. According to the WHO, the high
            proportion of infected medical staff can be explained by lack of the
            number of medical staff needed to manage such a large outbreak,
            shortages of protective equipment or improperly using what is
            available, and "the compassion that causes medical staff to work in
            isolation wards far beyond the number of hours recommended as
            safe.".<sup id="cite_ref-WHO_13-1" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-WHO-13"
                ><span>[</span>13<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            Comparing the present Ebola outbreak to some in the past, the WHO
            notes that many of the most recent districts in which epidemics have
            occurred were in remote areas where the transmission had been easier
            to track and break. This outbreak is different in that large cities
            have been affected as well, where tracking has been difficult and
            medical staff may not suspect Ebola disease when they make a
            diagnosis. Several infectious diseases endemic to West Africa, such
            as <a href="/wiki/Malaria" title="Malaria">malaria</a> and
            <a href="/wiki/Typhoid_fever" title="Typhoid fever">typhoid fever</a
            >, mimic the symptoms of Ebola disease, and doctors and nurses may
            see no need to take protective measures.<sup
              id="cite_ref-WHO_13-2"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-WHO-13"
                ><span>[</span>13<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            Also, without recent past experience with the disease, people have
            become intensely fearful and have in some cases attacked medical
            staff, believing that they cause the disease .<sup
              id="cite_ref-WHO_13-3"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-WHO-13"
                ><span>[</span>13<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            The WHO reports that in the hardest hit areas there have
            historically been only one or two doctors available to treat 100,000
            people, and these doctors are heavily concentrated in urban areas;
            the loss of so many health workers has made it difficult for the WHO
            to provide sufficient numbers of medical staff. Among the fatalities
            is Samuel Brisbane, a former advisor to the Liberian Ministry of
            Health and Social Welfare, described as "one of Liberia's most
            high-profile doctors."<sup
              id="cite_ref-guardian_27_July_2014_55-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-guardian_27_July_2014-55"
                ><span>[</span>55<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            In July, leading Ebola doctor
            <a href="/wiki/Sheik_Umar_Khan" title="Sheik Umar Khan"
              >Sheik Umar Khan</a
            >
            from Sierra Leone died in the outbreak. His death was followed by
            two more deaths in Sierra Leone: Modupe Cole, a senior physician at
            the country`s main referral facility,<sup
              id="cite_ref-56"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-56"><span>[</span>56<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            and Sahr Rogers, who worked in
            <a href="/wiki/Kenema" title="Kenema">Kenema</a>.<sup
              id="cite_ref-57"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-57"><span>[</span>57<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            <sup id="cite_ref-Dr._Khan_Daily_News_58-0" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-Dr._Khan_Daily_News-58"
                ><span>[</span>58<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-59"><span>[</span>59<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            <sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-60"><span>[</span>60<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            The African Union has launched an urgent initiative to recruit more
            health care workers from among its members.<sup
              id="cite_ref-WHO_13-4"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-WHO-13"
                ><span>[</span>13<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Treatment">Treatment</span></h2>
          <div class="thumb tright">
            <div class="thumbinner" style="width: 222px">
              <a href="/wiki/File:Monoclonal_antibodies3.jpg" class="image"
                ><img
                  alt=""
                  src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Monoclonal_antibodies3.jpg/220px-Monoclonal_antibodies3.jpg"
                  width="220"
                  height="147"
                  class="thumbimage"
                  srcset="
                    //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Monoclonal_antibodies3.jpg/330px-Monoclonal_antibodies3.jpg 1.5x,
                    //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Monoclonal_antibodies3.jpg/440px-Monoclonal_antibodies3.jpg 2x
                  "
                  data-file-width="1350"
                  data-file-height="900"
              /></a>
              <div class="thumbcaption">
                <div class="magnify">
                  <a
                    href="/wiki/File:Monoclonal_antibodies3.jpg"
                    class="internal"
                    title="Enlarge"
                  ></a>
                </div>
                Researchers looking at slides of cultures of cells that make
                monoclonal antibodies. These are grown in a lab and the
                researchers are analyzing the products to select the most
                promising of them.
              </div>
            </div>
          </div>
          <div class="hatnote boilerplate seealso">
            See also:
            <a
              href="/wiki/Ebola_virus_disease#Research"
              title="Ebola virus disease"
              >Research into the Ebola virus disease</a
            >
            and
            <a
              href="/wiki/Ebola_virus_disease#Treatment"
              title="Ebola virus disease"
              >Treatment of the Ebola virus disease</a
            >
          </div>
          <p>
            No proven Ebola virus-specific treatment exists as of August
            2014.<sup id="cite_ref-Choi-2013_61-0" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-Choi-2013-61"
                ><span>[</span>61<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            Treatment is primarily supportive in nature and includes minimizing
            invasive procedures, balancing fluids and
            <a href="/wiki/Electrolyte" title="Electrolyte">electrolytes</a> to
            counter
            <a href="/wiki/Dehydration" title="Dehydration">dehydration</a>,
            administration of
            <a
              href="/wiki/Anticoagulants"
              title="Anticoagulants"
              class="mw-redirect"
              >anticoagulants</a
            >
            early in infection to prevent or control
            <a
              href="/wiki/Disseminated_intravascular_coagulation"
              title="Disseminated intravascular coagulation"
              >disseminated intravascular coagulation</a
            >, administration of
            <a href="/wiki/Coagulation" title="Coagulation">procoagulants</a>
            late in infection to control
            <a href="/wiki/Bleeding" title="Bleeding">bleeding</a>,
            <a href="/wiki/Oxygen_therapy" title="Oxygen therapy"
              >maintaining oxygen</a
            >
            levels,
            <a href="/wiki/Pain_management" title="Pain management"
              >pain management</a
            >, and the use of medications to treat bacterial or fungal secondary
            infections.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-62"><span>[</span>62<span>]</span></a></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-63"><span>[</span>63<span>]</span></a></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-64"><span>[</span>64<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            Early treatment may increase the chance of survival.<sup
              id="cite_ref-65"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-65"><span>[</span>65<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
          </p>
          <h3>
            <span class="mw-headline" id="Level_of_care">Level of care</span>
          </h3>
          <p>
            In late August Médecins Sans Frontières (MSF) called the situation
            "chaotic" and the medical response "inadequate". They report that
            they have expanded their operations but have been unable to keep up
            with the rapidly increasing need for assistance which has forced
            them to reduce the level of care they are able to offer: "It is not
            currently possible, for example, to administer intravenous
            treatments." Calling the situation "an emergency within the
            emergency", MSF reports that many hospitals have had to shut down
            due to lack of staff or fears of the virus among patients and staff
            which has left people with other health problems without any care at
            all. Speaking from a remote region, a MSF worker said that a
            shortage of protective equipment was making the medical management
            of the disease difficult and that they had limited capacity to
            safely bury bodies.<sup
              id="cite_ref-doctorswithoutborders.org_66-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-doctorswithoutborders.org-66"
                ><span>[</span>66<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <h3>
            <span class="mw-headline" id="Experimental_treatments"
              >Experimental treatments</span
            >
          </h3>
          <p>
            The unavailability of treatments in the most-affected regions has
            spurred controversy, with some calling for experimental drugs to be
            made more widely available in Africa on a humanitarian basis, and
            others warning that making unproven drugs widely available would be
            unethical, especially in light of past experimentation conducted in
            developing countries by Western drug companies.<sup
              id="cite_ref-LAtimes_67-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-LAtimes-67"
                ><span>[</span>67<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-NYTwho_68-0" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-NYTwho-68"
                ><span>[</span>68<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            As a result of the controversy, an expert panel of the WHO endorsed
            the use of interventions with as-yet-unknown effects both for
            treatment and for prevention of Ebola, and also said that deciding
            which treatments should be used and how to distribute them equitably
            were matters that needed further discussion.<sup
              id="cite_ref-69"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-69"><span>[</span>69<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            A number of experimental treatments are being studied or will
            undergo trials proximately:<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-70"><span>[</span>70<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
          </p>
          <ul>
            <li>
              <a href="/wiki/ZMapp" title="ZMapp">ZMapp</a>, a monoclonal
              antibody vaccine. The limited supply of the drug has been used to
              treat a few individuals infected with the Ebola virus: two U.S.
              aid workers who contracted Ebola in Liberia were successfully
              treated after repatriation in an
              <a href="/wiki/Atlanta" title="Atlanta">Atlanta</a> hospital;<sup
                id="cite_ref-Zmapp_treatment_71-0"
                class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-Zmapp_treatment-71"
                  ><span>[</span>71<span>]</span></a
                ></sup
              >
              a Spanish priest was treated after repatriation but later died;
              and three infected African doctors, one of whom died in
              <a href="/wiki/Monrovia" title="Monrovia">Monrovia</a> on 24
              August, also received the experimental drug.<sup
                id="cite_ref-Zmapp_treatment_71-1"
                class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-Zmapp_treatment-71"
                  ><span>[</span>71<span>]</span></a
                ></sup
              >
            </li>
            <li>
              <a href="/wiki/TKM-Ebola" title="TKM-Ebola">TKM-Ebola</a>, an
              <a href="/wiki/RNA_interference" title="RNA interference"
                >RNA interference</a
              >
              drug.<sup id="cite_ref-NYTimes08082014_72-0" class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-NYTimes08082014-72"
                  ><span>[</span>72<span>]</span></a
                ></sup
              >
            </li>
            <li>
              <a href="/wiki/Favipiravir" title="Favipiravir">Favipiravir</a>, a
              drug approved in <a href="/wiki/Japan" title="Japan">Japan</a> for
              stockpiling against influenza pandemics.<sup
                id="cite_ref-73"
                class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-73"><span>[</span>73<span>]</span></a></sup
              >
              The drug appears to be useful in a mouse model of the disease<sup
                id="cite_ref-Gatherer_2014_74-0"
                class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-Gatherer_2014-74"
                  ><span>[</span>74<span>]</span></a
                ></sup
              ><sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-75"><span>[</span>75<span>]</span></a></sup
              >
              and Japan has offered to supply the drug if requested by the
              WHO.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-76"><span>[</span>76<span>]</span></a></sup
              >
            </li>
          </ul>
          <h2>
            <span class="mw-headline" id="Fatality_rate">Fatality rate</span>
          </h2>
          <div class="hatnote boilerplate seealso">
            See also:
            <a
              href="/wiki/Ebola_virus_disease#Prognosis"
              title="Ebola virus disease"
              >Ebola virus disease § Prognosis</a
            >
          </div>
          <p>
            According to a website for collaborative analysis and discussion
            about the Ebola emergence, as of 7 August attempts to create an
            accurate Case Fatality Rate (CFR) had been unreliable due to
            differences in testing policies, the inclusion of probable and
            suspected cases, and primarily the rate of new cases that have not
            run their course.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-77"><span>[</span>77<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            However, on 28 August, the WHO made their first overall case
            fatality rate estimate of 52%. It ranges from 42% in Sierra Leone to
            66% in Guinea.<sup
              id="cite_ref-Disease_Outbreak_News_78-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-Disease_Outbreak_News-78"
                ><span>[</span>78<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            Compared to previous Zaire strain outbreaks, this number is quite
            low. The twelve Zaire strain outbreaks since the first one reported
            in the Democratic Republic of Congo in 1976 have had an average CFR
            of about 76%. Even the
            <a href="/wiki/Sudan_ebolavirus" title="Sudan ebolavirus"
              >Sudan ebolavirus</a
            >
            species, known to be less virulent than the Zaire species of the
            <a href="/wiki/Ebola_virus" title="Ebola virus">Ebola virus</a>, has
            had an average CFR of about 57%.<sup
              id="cite_ref-Ebola_media_centre_fact_sheets_79-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-Ebola_media_centre_fact_sheets-79"
                ><span>[</span>79<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <h2>
            <span class="mw-headline" id="Economic_effects"
              >Economic effects</span
            >
          </h2>
          <p>
            In addition to the loss of life, the outbreak is having a number of
            significant economic impacts.
          </p>
          <ul>
            <li>
              Markets and shops are closing, due to travel restrictions,
              <a href="/wiki/Cordon_sanitaire" title="Cordon sanitaire"
                >cordon sanitaire</a
              >, or fear of human contact, leading to loss of income for
              producers and traders.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-80"><span>[</span>80<span>]</span></a></sup
              >
            </li>
            <li>
              Movement of people away from affected areas has disturbed
              agricultural activities.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-81"><span>[</span>81<span>]</span></a></sup
              ><sup id="cite_ref-rappler1_82-0" class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-rappler1-82"
                  ><span>[</span>82<span>]</span></a
                ></sup
              >
            </li>
            <li>
              Tourism is directly impacted in affected countries.<sup
                id="cite_ref-83"
                class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-83"><span>[</span>83<span>]</span></a></sup
              >
              Other countries in Africa which are not directly affected by the
              virus have also reported adverse effects on tourism<sup
                id="cite_ref-84"
                class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-84"><span>[</span>84<span>]</span></a></sup
              >
            </li>
            <li>
              Foreign mining companies have withdrawn non-essential personnel,
              deferred new investment, and cut back operations.<sup
                id="cite_ref-rappler1_82-1"
                class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-rappler1-82"
                  ><span>[</span>82<span>]</span></a
                ></sup
              ><sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-85"><span>[</span>85<span>]</span></a></sup
              ><sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-86"><span>[</span>86<span>]</span></a></sup
              >
            </li>
            <li>
              Many airlines have experienced reduced traffic. Some airlines have
              suspended flights to the area.<sup
                id="cite_ref-87"
                class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-87"><span>[</span>87<span>]</span></a></sup
              >
            </li>
            <li>
              Forecasts of economic growth have been reduced.<sup
                id="cite_ref-88"
                class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-88"><span>[</span>88<span>]</span></a></sup
              >
              An initial World Bank-IMF assessment for Guinea projects a full
              percentage point fall in GDP growth from 4.5 percent to 3.5
              percent<sup id="cite_ref-worldbank1_89-0" class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-worldbank1-89"
                  ><span>[</span>89<span>]</span></a
                ></sup
              >
            </li>
            <li>
              The outbreak is straining the finances of governments, with Sierra
              Leone using Treasury bills to fund the fight against the virus<sup
                id="cite_ref-90"
                class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-90"><span>[</span>90<span>]</span></a></sup
              >
            </li>
            <li>
              The U.N.
              <a
                href="/wiki/Food_and_Agriculture_Organisation"
                title="Food and Agriculture Organisation"
                class="mw-redirect"
                >Food and Agriculture Organisation</a
              >
              (FAO) has warned that the outbreak could endanger harvest and food
              security in West Africa .<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"
                ><a href="#cite_note-91"><span>[</span>91<span>]</span></a></sup
              >
            </li>
          </ul>
          <h2>
            <span
              class="mw-headline"
              id="Unrelated_outbreak_in_the_Democratic_Republic_of_Congo"
              >Unrelated outbreak in the Democratic Republic of Congo</span
            >
          </h2>
          <table
            class="infobox vevent"
            cellspacing="3"
            style="border-spacing: 3px; width: 22em"
          >
            <caption class="summary">
              Democratic Republic of Congo-2014
            </caption>
            <tr>
              <td
                colspan="2"
                style="text-align: center; border-bottom: #aaa solid 1px"
              >
                <a href="/wiki/File:DRC_Ebola_Map.png" class="image"
                  ><img
                    alt="DRC Ebola Map.png"
                    src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/DRC_Ebola_Map.png/240px-DRC_Ebola_Map.png"
                    width="240"
                    height="217"
                    srcset="
                      //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/DRC_Ebola_Map.png/360px-DRC_Ebola_Map.png 1.5x,
                      //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/DRC_Ebola_Map.png/480px-DRC_Ebola_Map.png 2x
                    "
                    data-file-width="805"
                    data-file-height="729"
                /></a>
                <div>
                  DRC Ebola area as of 2 September 2014<sup
                    class="plainlinks noprint asof-tag update"
                    style="display: none"
                    ><a
                      class="external text"
                      href="//en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ebola_virus_epidemic_in_West_Africa&amp;action=edit"
                      >[update]</a
                    ></sup
                  ><sup id="cite_ref-DRC_Ebola_Map_92-0" class="reference"
                    ><a href="#cite_note-DRC_Ebola_Map-92"
                      ><span>[</span>92<span>]</span></a
                    ></sup
                  >
                </div>
              </td>
            </tr>
            <tr>
              <th
                colspan="2"
                class="navbox-abovebelow"
                style="text-align: center"
              >
                Casualties
              </th>
            </tr>
            <tr>
              <td colspan="2" style="text-align: center; text-align: left">
                <div class="plainlist" style="margin-left: 0em">
                  <ul>
                    <li>
                      <b>Cases / Deaths</b>
                      <small
                        >(as of 2 September 2014<sup
                          class="plainlinks noprint asof-tag update"
                          style="display: none"
                          ><a
                            class="external text"
                            href="//en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ebola_virus_epidemic_in_West_Africa&amp;action=edit"
                            >[update]</a
                          ></sup
                        >)</small
                      ><sup
                        id="cite_ref-DRC_Ebola_case_update_93-0"
                        class="reference"
                        ><a href="#cite_note-DRC_Ebola_case_update-93"
                          ><span>[</span>93<span>]</span></a
                        ></sup
                      >
                    </li>
                    <li>
                      <span class="flagicon"
                        ><img
                          alt=""
                          src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Flag_of_the_Democratic_Republic_of_the_Congo.svg/20px-Flag_of_the_Democratic_Republic_of_the_Congo.svg.png"
                          width="20"
                          height="15"
                          class="thumbborder"
                          srcset="
                            //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Flag_of_the_Democratic_Republic_of_the_Congo.svg/31px-Flag_of_the_Democratic_Republic_of_the_Congo.svg.png 1.5x,
                            //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6f/Flag_of_the_Democratic_Republic_of_the_Congo.svg/40px-Flag_of_the_Democratic_Republic_of_the_Congo.svg.png 2x
                          "
                          data-file-width="800"
                          data-file-height="600"
                        />&#160;</span
                      ><a
                        href="/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_the_Congo"
                        title="Democratic Republic of the Congo"
                        >DR Congo</a
                      >: 59 / 32
                    </li>
                  </ul>
                </div>
              </td>
            </tr>
          </table>
          <p>
            On 20 August, several people, including four health care workers,
            were reported to have died of Ebola-like symptoms in the remote
            northern
            <a href="/wiki/%C3%89quateur" title="Équateur">Équateur</a>
            province, a province that lies about 750 miles north of the capital
            <a href="/wiki/Kinshasa" title="Kinshasa">Kinshasa</a>.<sup
              id="cite_ref-94"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-94"><span>[</span>94<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            By 21 August, 13 people were reported to have died with similar
            symptoms. On 22 August, a
            <a
              href="/wiki/World_Health_Organization"
              title="World Health Organization"
              >World Health Organization</a
            >
            (WHO) official and
            <a
              href="/wiki/M%C3%A9decins_Sans_Fronti%C3%A8res"
              title="Médecins Sans Frontières"
              >Médecins Sans Frontières</a
            >
            (MSF) announced that it was too soon to tell whether Ebola had
            caused the deaths.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-95"><span>[</span>95<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            Blood samples were sent for testing and on 24 August it was reported
            that the samples were positive for Ebola; one tested positive for
            the
            <a href="/wiki/Sudan_ebolavirus" title="Sudan ebolavirus"
              >Sudan ebolavirus</a
            >, which is a different species of ebolavirus than the one
            responsible for the West African outbreak; the other tested positive
            for a mixture of the Sudanese and
            <a href="/wiki/Ebola_virus" title="Ebola virus">Zaire species</a>,
            the species that is responsible for the present outbreak in West
            Africa.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-96"><span>[</span>96<span>]</span></a></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-Ebola_Outbreak_in_DRC_97-0" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-Ebola_Outbreak_in_DRC-97"
                ><span>[</span>97<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On 26 August, the Équateur Province Ministry of Health confirmed an
            outbreak of Ebola to the WHO.<sup
              id="cite_ref-DRC_Ebola_case_98-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-DRC_Ebola_case-98"
                ><span>[</span>98<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            The initial case was a woman from Ikanamongo Village who became ill
            with symptoms of Ebola after she had butchered a bush animal that
            her husband had killed. She was treated in a private clinic in Isaka
            Village, but on 11 August she died of a then-unidentified
            <a
              href="/wiki/Hemorrhagic_fever"
              title="Hemorrhagic fever"
              class="mw-redirect"
              >hemorrhagic fever</a
            >. The following week, relatives of the woman, several health-care
            workers who had treated the woman, and individuals that they had
            been in contact with came down with similar symptoms. Five health
            care workers subsequently died.<sup
              id="cite_ref-DRC_Ebola_case_98-1"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-DRC_Ebola_case-98"
                ><span>[</span>98<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On 26 August, the WHO reported: "Between 28 July and 18 August, a
            total of 24 suspected cases of Ebola virus, including 13 deaths,
            have been noted. Samples have been sent to laboratories in Kinshasa
            and in Gabon for confirmation of EVD and to identify the strain. The
            index case and the 80 contacts have no history of travel to the
            EVD-affected countries in West Africa (Guinea, Liberia, Nigeria, or
            Sierra Leone) or history of contact with individuals from the
            affected areas. At this time, it is believed that the outbreak in
            DRC is unrelated to the ongoing outbreak in West Africa."<sup
              id="cite_ref-DRC_Ebola_case_98-2"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-DRC_Ebola_case-98"
                ><span>[</span>98<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On Tuesday 2 September the head of communication for the WHO, Eugene
            Kambambi, told Agence France-Presse (AFP) that there are currently
            31 deaths in the Northern
            <a href="/wiki/Boende" title="Boende">Boende</a> area in the
            province of Équateur. Kambambi also added that there were "53
            confirmed, suspected or likely cases". Currently 185 other contacts
            cases are being monitored for any signs of the disease. MSF have
            also told AFP on Monday that they are in the process of setting up a
            clinic in the area to treat Ebola patients.<sup
              id="cite_ref-DRC_Ebola_case_spread_99-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-DRC_Ebola_case_spread-99"
                ><span>[</span>99<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On 2 September, the World Health Organization confirmed that the
            current strain of the virus in the Boende District is the Zaire
            Ebola species. This strain is common in the country and similar to
            the 1995 <a href="/wiki/Kikwit" title="Kikwit">Kikwit</a> outbreak
            in the DRC. The samples were tested positive for this strain by
            <a href="/wiki/Gabon" title="Gabon">Gabon</a>’s Centre International
            de Recherches Médicales in
            <a href="/wiki/Franceville" title="Franceville">Franceville</a>. The
            virology results and epidemiological findings indicates no
            connection to the current epidemic in West Africa Region or Nigeria.
            The variant is different from those tested in the epidemic
            region.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-100"><span>[</span>100<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On 6 September the Health Minister, Felix Kabange Numbi, released a
            press statement raising the death toll to 32 from possible or
            confirmed Ebola cases. The total possible infected cases was raised
            to 59 as well. The outbreak is still contained to the Boende region,
            with 336 contact cases being tracked.<sup
              id="cite_ref-DRC_Ebola_case_update_93-1"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-DRC_Ebola_case_update-93"
                ><span>[</span>93<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <h2>
            <span class="mw-headline" id="Response_by_country"
              >Response by country</span
            >
          </h2>
          <h3>
            <span
              class="mw-headline"
              id="Countries_with_active_local_transmission"
              >Countries with active local transmission</span
            >
          </h3>
          <table
            class="infobox vevent"
            cellspacing="3"
            style="border-spacing: 3px; width: 22em"
          >
            <caption class="summary">
              Treatment facilities in West Africa
            </caption>
            <tr>
              <td
                colspan="2"
                style="text-align: center; border-bottom: #aaa solid 1px"
              >
                <a href="/wiki/File:WHO_ebola_response_map.jpg" class="image"
                  ><img
                    alt="WHO ebola response map.jpg"
                    src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/95/WHO_ebola_response_map.jpg/300px-WHO_ebola_response_map.jpg"
                    width="300"
                    height="300"
                    srcset="
                      //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/95/WHO_ebola_response_map.jpg/450px-WHO_ebola_response_map.jpg 1.5x,
                      //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/95/WHO_ebola_response_map.jpg/600px-WHO_ebola_response_map.jpg 2x
                    "
                    data-file-width="1290"
                    data-file-height="1288"
                /></a>
                <div>
                  Treatment facilities and responses in the West African Ebola
                  region as of 5 September 2014<sup
                    class="plainlinks noprint asof-tag update"
                    style="display: none"
                    ><a
                      class="external text"
                      href="//en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ebola_virus_epidemic_in_West_Africa&amp;action=edit"
                      >[update]</a
                    ></sup
                  ><sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"
                    ><a href="#cite_note-101"
                      ><span>[</span>101<span>]</span></a
                    ></sup
                  >
                </div>
              </td>
            </tr>
          </table>
          <h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Guinea">Guinea</span></h4>
          <p>
            The border between Guinea and Liberia remained open in April;
            Guinea's ambassador in Monrovia noted his government's belief that
            efforts to fight the disease directly would be more effective than
            closing the border.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-102"><span>[</span>102<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            In early August 2014, Guinea closed its borders with both Sierra
            Leone and Liberia to help contain the spreading of the disease, as
            more new cases were being reported in those countries than in
            Guinea.<sup id="cite_ref-GuineaBorders_103-0" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-GuineaBorders-103"
                ><span>[</span>103<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Liberia">Liberia</span></h4>
          <p>
            By 23 July, the Liberian health ministry began to implement a
            strategic plan in line with the
            <a href="/wiki/Accra" title="Accra">Accra</a> meeting's conclusions
            to improve the country's response to the outbreak.<sup
              id="cite_ref-WHO_23_July_2014_104-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-WHO_23_July_2014-104"
                ><span>[</span>104<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On 27 July,
            <a href="/wiki/Ellen_Johnson_Sirleaf" title="Ellen Johnson Sirleaf"
              >Ellen Johnson Sirleaf</a
            >, the Liberian president, announced that Liberia would close its
            borders, with the exception of a few crossing points, such as the
            country's principal airport, where screening centres would be
            established, and the worst-affected areas in the country would be
            placed under quarantine.<sup
              id="cite_ref-BBC_28_July_2014_49-1"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-BBC_28_July_2014-49"
                ><span>[</span>49<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            <a href="/wiki/Association_football" title="Association football"
              >Football</a
            >
            events were banned, because large gatherings and the nature of the
            sport increase transmission risks.<sup
              id="cite_ref-105"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-105"><span>[</span>105<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            Three days after the borders were closed, Sirleaf announced the
            closure of all schools nationwide, including the
            <a href="/wiki/University_of_Liberia" title="University of Liberia"
              >University of Liberia</a
            >,<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-106"><span>[</span>106<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            and a few communities were to be quarantined.<sup
              id="cite_ref-107"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-107"><span>[</span>107<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            Sirleaf declared a state of emergency on 6 August, partly because
            the disease's weakening of the health care system has the potential
            to reduce the system's ability to treat routine diseases such as
            malaria; she noted that the state of emergency might require the
            "suspensions of certain rights and privileges."<sup
              id="cite_ref-108"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-108"><span>[</span>108<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            On the same day, the
            <a
              href="/wiki/National_Elections_Commission_of_Liberia"
              title="National Elections Commission of Liberia"
              >National Elections Commission</a
            >
            announced that it would be unable to conduct the scheduled October
            2014
            <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Liberia" title="Senate of Liberia"
              >senatorial</a
            >
            election and requested postponement,<sup
              id="cite_ref-109"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-109"><span>[</span>109<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            one week after the leaders of various opposition parties had
            publicly taken different sides on the question.<sup
              id="cite_ref-110"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-110"><span>[</span>110<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On 18 August, a mob of residents from
            <a href="/wiki/West_Point,_Monrovia" title="West Point, Monrovia"
              >West Point</a
            >, an impoverished area of Monrovia, descended upon a local Ebola
            clinic to protest its presence. The protesters turned violent,
            threatening the caretakers, removing the infected patients, and
            looting the clinic of its supplies, including blood-stained bed
            sheets and mattresses. Police and aid workers expressed fear that
            this would lead to mass infections of Ebola in West Point.<sup
              id="cite_ref-autogenerated5_111-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated5-111"
                ><span>[</span>111<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-112"><span>[</span>112<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            The Liberian government has since quarantined the entirety of West
            Point, despite riots, and issued a curfew state-wide.<sup
              id="cite_ref-nbcnews1_113-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-nbcnews1-113"
                ><span>[</span>113<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-114"><span>[</span>114<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            On 22 August it was reported that violence had again broken out
            after the military fired on protesting crowds.<sup
              id="cite_ref-news3_115-0"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-news3-115"
                ><span>[</span>115<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On 22 August, officials declared that two people in Sinoe province
            had died from Ebola. This area was the last Ebola free area in
            Liberia. With 624 deaths recorded to date, the country has seen the
            highest death toll of all affected areas.<sup
              id="cite_ref-news3_115-1"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-news3-115"
                ><span>[</span>115<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On 26 August,
            <a href="/wiki/Tom_Frieden" title="Tom Frieden">Tom Frieden</a>,
            director of the U.S.
            <a
              href="/wiki/Centers_for_Disease_Control"
              title="Centers for Disease Control"
              class="mw-redirect"
              >Centers for Disease Control</a
            >, said at a meeting attended by the Liberian President, Ellen
            Johnson Sirleaf, “Lots of hard work is happening. Lots of good
            things are happening, but the virus still has the upper hand.”
            President Sirleaf's office said in a statement that any top
            government officials who defied her orders to remain in Liberia or
            to return from any trips outside the country had been fired; how
            many or who had been dismissed was unclear.<sup
              id="cite_ref-116"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-116"><span>[</span>116<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            The quarantine blockade in the West Point area, imposed by the
            Liberian president was lifted on Saturday 30 August. The Information
            Minister, Lewis Brown, said that this step was taken to ease efforts
            to screen, test and treat residents for the disease. He added that
            this step does not mean the area is Ebola free. The quarantine
            blockade was in place since 19 August.<sup
              id="cite_ref-117"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-117"><span>[</span>117<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On 30 August, Liberia's
            <a
              href="/wiki/Port_Authority"
              title="Port Authority"
              class="mw-redirect"
              >Port Authority</a
            >
            have cancelled all "shore passes" for sailors from ships coming into
            the country's four seaports. No one will be allowed to disembark
            from any commercial ships while in port. Three added screening
            points are in place for persons going on board a ship; these include
            the port gate, the security desk and the pier.<sup
              id="cite_ref-118"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-118"><span>[</span>118<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
          </p>
          <h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Nigeria">Nigeria</span></h4>
          <table
            class="infobox vevent"
            cellspacing="3"
            style="border-spacing: 3px; width: 22em"
          >
            <caption class="summary">
              Nigeria Ebola areas-2014
            </caption>
            <tr>
              <td
                colspan="2"
                style="text-align: center; border-bottom: #aaa solid 1px"
              >
                <a href="/wiki/File:Nigeria_Map_Ebola_2014.png" class="image"
                  ><img
                    alt="Nigeria Map Ebola 2014.png"
                    src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Nigeria_Map_Ebola_2014.png/240px-Nigeria_Map_Ebola_2014.png"
                    width="240"
                    height="177"
                    srcset="
                      //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Nigeria_Map_Ebola_2014.png/360px-Nigeria_Map_Ebola_2014.png 1.5x,
                      //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Nigeria_Map_Ebola_2014.png/480px-Nigeria_Map_Ebola_2014.png 2x
                    "

                    data-file-height="646"
                /></a>
                <div>
                  Nigeria Situation Map as of 5 September 2014<sup

                    ><a

                      >[update]</a
                    ></sup
                  ><sup id="cite_ref-WHO-ebola-rpt_119-0" class="reference"
                    ><a href="#cite_note-WHO-ebola-rpt-119"
                      ><span>[</span>119<span>]</span></a
                    ></sup
                  >
                </div>
              </td>
            </tr>
            <tr>
              <th scope="row" style="text-align: left">Date</th>
              <td style="text-align: left">
                July 2014 – present<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"
                  ><a href="#cite_note-120"
                    ><span>[</span>120<span>]</span></a
                  ></sup
                >
              </td>
            </tr>
            <tr>
              <th
                colspan="2"
                class="navbox-abovebelow"
                style="text-align: center"
              >
                Casualties
              </th>
            </tr>
            <tr>
              <td colspan="2" style="text-align: center; text-align: left">
                <div class="plainlist" style="margin-left: 0em">
                  <ul>
                    <li>
                      <b>Cases / Deaths</b>
                      <small
                        >(as of 5 September 2014<sup
                          class="plainlinks noprint asof-tag update"
                          style="display: none"
                          ><a
                            class="external text"
                            href="//en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ebola_virus_epidemic_in_West_Africa&amp;action=edit"
                            >[update]</a
                          ></sup
                        >)</small
                      ><sup
                        id="cite_ref-Ebola_Outbreak_total_7-1"
                        class="reference"
                        ><a href="#cite_note-Ebola_Outbreak_total-7"
                          ><span>[</span>7<span>]</span></a
                        ></sup
                      >
                    </li>
                    <li>
                      <span class="flagicon"
                        ><img
                          alt=""
                          src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Flag_of_Nigeria.svg/23px-Flag_of_Nigeria.svg.png"
                          width="23"
                          height="12"
                          class="thumbborder"
                          srcset="
                            //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Flag_of_Nigeria.svg/35px-Flag_of_Nigeria.svg.png 1.5x,
                            //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Flag_of_Nigeria.svg/46px-Flag_of_Nigeria.svg.png 2x
                          "
                          data-file-width="2400"
                          data-file-height="1200"
                        />&#160;</span
                      ><a href="/wiki/Nigeria" title="Nigeria">Nigeria</a>: 22 /
                      8
                    </li>
                  </ul>
                </div>
              </td>
            </tr>
          </table>
          <p>
            The first reported Ebola case in Nigeria was an imported case of a
            Liberian-American, Patrick Sawyer, who travelled by air from Liberia
            and became violently ill upon arriving in the city of Lagos. Sawyer
            died five days later, on 25 July. In response, the Nigerian
            government observed all of Sawyer's contacts for signs of infection
            and increased surveillance at all entry points to the country;
            health officials were placed at entry points to conduct tests on
            people arriving in the country.<sup
              id="cite_ref-121"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-121"><span>[</span>121<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            On 19 August, it was reported that the doctor who treated Sawyer,
            <a href="/wiki/Ameyo_Adadevoh" title="Ameyo Adadevoh"
              >Ameyo Adadevoh</a
            >, had also died of Ebola disease.<sup
              id="cite_ref-reuters1_34-1"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-reuters1-34"
                ><span>[</span>34<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-122"><span>[</span>122<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            Adadevoh, a descendant of
            <a href="/wiki/Herbert_Macaulay" title="Herbert Macaulay"
              >Herbert Macaulay</a
            ><sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-123"><span>[</span>123<span>]</span></a></sup
            ><sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-124"><span>[</span>124<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            and
            <a href="/wiki/Samuel_Ajayi_Crowther" title="Samuel Ajayi Crowther"
              >Samuel Ajayi Crowther</a
            ><sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-125"><span>[</span>125<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
            was posthumously praised for preventing the index case (Sawyer) from
            leaving the hospital at the time of diagnosis, thereby playing a key
            role in curbing the spread of the virus in Nigeria. On 6 August,
            Nigerian authorities confirmed the Ebola death of a nurse who had
            also treated Sawyer.<sup
              id="cite_ref-theguardian1_35-1"
              class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-theguardian1-35"
                ><span>[</span>35<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            In July, <a href="/wiki/Arik_Air" title="Arik Air">Arik Air</a>,
            Nigeria's main airline, stopped flights to Liberia and Sierra
            Leone.<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_28_July_2014_49-2" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-BBC_28_July_2014-49"
                ><span>[</span>49<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On 9 August, the
            <a
              href="/wiki/Nigerian_National_Health_Research_Ethics_Committee"
              title="Nigerian National Health Research Ethics Committee"
              >Nigerian National Health Research Ethics Committee</a
            >, the organization regulating research ethics in the country,
            issued a statement waiving the regular administrative requirements
            that limit the international shipment of any biological samples out
            of Nigeria.<sup id="cite_ref-NHREC_126-0" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-NHREC-126"
                ><span>[</span>126<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
            The statement also supports the use of non-validated treatments
            without prior review and approval by a health
            <a
              href="/wiki/Institutional_review_board"
              title="Institutional review board"
              >research ethics committee</a
            >.<sup id="cite_ref-NHREC_126-1" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-NHREC-126"
                ><span>[</span>126<span>]</span></a
              ></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On 14 August, the Nigerian government said
            <a href="/wiki/Aliko_Dangote" title="Aliko Dangote"
              >Aliko Dangote</a
            >
            had donated 150 million naira to halt the spread of the Ebola virus
            outbreak.<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"
              ><a href="#cite_note-127"><span>[</span>127<span>]</span></a></sup
            >
          </p>
          <p>
            On 19 August, the Commissioner of health in Lagos announced that
